# A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula

Chenruo Liu    Yijun Dong    Yiqiu Shen    Qi Lei Affiliation: New York
University

###### Abstract

Iterative self-improvement fine-tunes an autoregressive large language
model (LLM) on reward-verified outputs generated by the LLM itself. In
contrast to the empirical success of self-improvement, the theoretical
foundation of this generative, iterative procedure in a practical,
finite-sample setting remains limited. We make progress toward this goal
by modeling each round of self-improvement as maximum-likelihood
fine-tuning on a reward-filtered distribution and deriving finite-sample
guarantees for the expected reward. Our analysis reveals an explicit
feedback loop where better models accept more data per iteration,
supporting sustained self-improvement while explaining eventual
saturation of such improvement. Adopting a task-centric view by
considering reasoning tasks with multiple difficulty levels, we further
prove quantifiable conditions on model initialization, task difficulty,
and sample budget where easy-to-hard curricula provably achieve better
guarantees than training on fixed mixtures of tasks. Our analyses are
validated through Monte-Carlo simulations and experiments spanning a
synthetic graph-based reasoning task and multiple standard mathematical
reasoning benchmarks.

^(†)^(†)footnotetext: Accepted at the International Conference on
Machine Learning (ICML) 2026.

## 1 Introduction

Conditioned on strong pre-training, modern large language models (LLMs)
increasingly acquire their unprecedented reasoning skills during
post-training not only from human-annotated supervision but also via
_iterative self-improvement_—a supervision-free loop where the model
iteratively generates candidate answers for questions from the
downstream task and then gets fine-tuned on curated question-answer
pairs that pass certain external verifications \[[36](#bib.bib1),
[37](#bib.bib2), [42](#bib.bib28), [20](#bib.bib4), [21](#bib.bib5),
[25](#bib.bib6), [44](#bib.bib7), [15](#bib.bib8)\]. A closely related
practice is to schedule these self-improvement iterations using an
_easy-to-hard curriculum_, where gradually shifting the downstream
question distribution toward more challenging instances often improves
the final performance \[[25](#bib.bib6), [18](#bib.bib24),
[19](#bib.bib25)\].

From a theoretical perspective, the empirical success of
self-improvement is arguably surprising because the training data is not
exogenous, i.e., the candidate solutions are generated by the model
itself. Recent theories on self-improvement have made progress on
clarifying why learning such endogenous data succeeds without violating
the data processing inequality \[[27](#bib.bib31)\], mainly from the
model evolution perspective. For example, [Huang et al.
\[17\]](#bib.bib9) casts self-improvement as a form of probability-mass
“sharpening”; while [Sun et al. \[30\]](#bib.bib10) takes a
solver–verifier-gap view of the learning dynamics at the continuous
limit. However, as a post-training strategy, the success of
self-improvement is highly dependent on the interaction between the
pre-trained model and the downstream task. In addition, the discrete,
multi-step iterations are critical for the appealing empirical gain of
self-improvement. What remains theoretically under-specified for such
self-improvement pipelines is a finite-sample, task-centric account that
answers two practical questions:

![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzEucDMucGljMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjQ5Ljc5IiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgdmVyc2lvbj0iMS4xIiB2aWV3Ym94PSIwIDAgNTIwIDQ5Ljc5IiB3aWR0aD0iNTIwIj48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNHB0IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDQ5Ljc5KSBtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAwKSI+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzQwNDA0MDsiIGZpbGw9IiM0MDQwNDAiIGZpbGwtb3BhY2l0eT0iMS4wIj48cGF0aCBzdHlsZT0ic3Ryb2tlOm5vbmUiIGQ9Ik0gMCA1LjkxIEwgMCA0My44OCBDIDAgNDcuMTQgMi42NCA0OS43OSA1LjkxIDQ5Ljc5IEwgNTE0LjEgNDkuNzkgQyA1MTcuMzYgNDkuNzkgNTIwIDQ3LjE0IDUyMCA0My44OCBMIDUyMCA1LjkxIEMgNTIwIDIuNjQgNTE3LjM2IDAgNTE0LjEgMCBMIDUuOTEgMCBDIDIuNjQgMCAwIDIuNjQgMCA1LjkxIFoiIC8+PC9nPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNGMkYyRjI7IiBmaWxsPSIjRjJGMkYyIiBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCI+PHBhdGggc3R5bGU9InN0cm9rZTpub25lIiBkPSJNIDEuOTcgNS45MSBMIDEuOTcgNDMuODggQyAxLjk3IDQ2LjA1IDMuNzMgNDcuODIgNS45MSA0Ny44MiBMIDUxNC4xIDQ3LjgyIEMgNTE2LjI3IDQ3LjgyIDUxOC4wMyA0Ni4wNSA1MTguMDMgNDMuODggTCA1MTguMDMgNS45MSBDIDUxOC4wMyAzLjczIDUxNi4yNyAxLjk3IDUxNC4xIDEuOTcgTCA1LjkxIDEuOTcgQyAzLjczIDEuOTcgMS45NyAzLjczIDEuOTcgNS45MSBaIiAvPjwvZz48ZyBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIDEwLjA2IDI5LjM1KSI+PGZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3Qgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZvLXdpZHRoOjM2LjEzZW07LS1sdHgtZm8taGVpZ2h0OjAuNzVlbTstLWx0eC1mby1kZXB0aDoxLjM5ZW07Zm9udC1zaXplOjEwcHQ7IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjI5LjY3IiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAxMC4zOCkiIHdpZHRoPSI0OTkuOTQiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250YWluZXIiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250ZW50Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IlMxLnAzLnBpYzEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9pbmxpbmUtYmxvY2sgbHR4X21pbmlwYWdlIGx0eF9hbGlnbl9ib3R0b20iIHN0eWxlPSJ3aWR0aDozNi4xM2VtOyI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTMS5wMy5waWMxLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9wIGx0eF9hbGlnbl9jZW50ZXIiPjxlbSBpZD0iUzEucDMucGljMS4xLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9lbXBoIGx0eF9mb250X2l0YWxpYyIgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZnLWNvbG ##### Our contributions.

We provide theory-grounded answers to the above questions that closely
match self-improvement in practice, which can be summarized as follows:

- •
  A task-centric framework unveiling when does (multi-step)
  self-improvement happen on a single task. Toward the first question,
  we model each self-improvement iteration as maximum-likelihood
  fine-tuning on a reward-filtered distribution induced by the model’s
  own generation and an external verifier
  ([Section 3](#S3 "3 Problem Setup and Notation ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")).
  On a single task, our expected reward lower bounds for
  single/multi-step self-improvement highlight a feedback loop in which
  the filtered distributions of better models have more data per
  iteration being accepted, effectively increasing the training set
  size. This lens further clarifies the effects of model initialization,
  task difficulty, and finite sample sizes on the sustained
  self-improvement and its eventual saturation
  ([Section 4](#S4 "4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")).
- •
  Across multiple tasks, moderate separation in task difficulties is
  essential for effective easy-to-hard curricula. Toward the second
  question, we compare easy-to-hard scheduling across self-improvement
  iterations with training on a fixed-mixture baseline under the same
  budget. We derive quantifiable conditions under which easy-to-hard
  curricula enjoy strictly better expected reward lower bounds. The
  analysis unveils three levers for the effectiveness of easy-to-hard
  scheduling in self-improvement: moderate separation of task
  difficulties, a critical sample budget, and model initialization
  ([Section 5](#S5 "5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")).
- •
  Empirical validation across diverse mathematical reasoning tasks. We
  validate the analysis for both questions through experiments on a
  synthetic graph-based reasoning task and multiple standard
  mathematical reasoning benchmarks, including GSM8K \[[4](#bib.bib41)\]
  and DeepMind Mathematics \[[26](#bib.bib40)\]
  ([Section 6](#S6 "6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")),
  and complement these experiments with Monte-Carlo simulations that
  directly visualize the evolution of the expected reward lower bound
  across self-improvement iterations
  ([Section 5](#S5 "5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")).

## 2 Related Work

##### Self-improvement for LLM mathematical reasoning.

In the realm of LLMs, self-improvement broadly refers to a family of
procedures in which a LLM produces its own supervision signal and then
leverages this signal to improve its capabilities. Empirically, many
pipelines for reasoning tasks, especially mathematical reasoning,
instantiate this idea via an iterative generate-and-filter loop: the
model generates one or multiple candidate solutions for each problem,
retains a subset of correct solutions, and then fine-tunes on the
retained solutions to enhance performance \[[36](#bib.bib1),
[37](#bib.bib2), [42](#bib.bib28), [16](#bib.bib3), [20](#bib.bib4),
[21](#bib.bib5), [25](#bib.bib6), [44](#bib.bib7), [15](#bib.bib8)\].
While existing approaches vary in implementation details (e.g.,
incorporating long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning \[[21](#bib.bib5)\],
tactic annotations \[[37](#bib.bib2)\], code-augmented CoT data
\[[15](#bib.bib8)\], or reflection steps \[[44](#bib.bib7)\]), their
overall framework shares the same spirit of bootstrapping from
model-generated attempts filtered by an explicit correctness signal.
Additionally, a growing line of work \[[25](#bib.bib6),
[18](#bib.bib24), [19](#bib.bib25)\] shows that combining
self-improvement with an explicit easy-to-hard curriculum across rounds
can further strengthen model performance.

##### Theoretical Understanding of LLM self-improvement.

While LLM self-improvement has shown strong empirical success, another
line of work seeks to understand its underlying mechanisms from a
theoretical perspective. [Huang et al. \[17\]](#bib.bib9) formalizes
self-improvement as a consequence of a sharpening mechanism, which
encourages the model to place larger probability mass on higher-quality
sequences. From a different perspective, [Mohri et al.
\[23\]](#bib.bib19) studies self-improvement through the lens of
coherence. [Yang et al. \[40\]](#bib.bib39) investigates the impact of
budget allocation policies across self-improvement iterations. [Sun et
al. \[30\]](#bib.bib10) models the training dynamics of self-improvement
via the solver-verifier gap. In contrast to these works, our theoretical
modeling of self-improvement is more closely aligned with mathematical
reasoning settings, i.e., binary and verifiable rewards together with
reject sampling for data collection. More importantly, our analysis
characterizes multi-step iterative self-improvement and further
incorporates its interaction with easy-to-hard curricula.

##### Self-distillation, self-consuming loops, and model collapse.

First, several theoretical works on self-distillation analyze training a
model with supervision signals generated by the model itself
\[[22](#bib.bib20), [5](#bib.bib18), [24](#bib.bib12)\]. In contrast, we
study LLM self-improvement for generative reasoning, which is typically
outside the scope of standard self-distillation analyses (e.g., linear
predictors). Second, another related line of work concerns
self-consuming loops in generative models, a failure mode where
repeatedly training on generated data can degrade performance and even
lead to model collapse \[[10](#bib.bib13), [9](#bib.bib17)\]. Recent
theory shows that such degradation can be mitigated under suitable
mechanisms \[[13](#bib.bib14), [12](#bib.bib15), [7](#bib.bib16),
[6](#bib.bib22), [8](#bib.bib23), [9](#bib.bib17)\]. While the objective
of preventing model collapse in this literature differs from LLM
self-improvement, \[[7](#bib.bib16)\] is particularly relevant to our
work as it explicitly characterizes how curated data can optimize a
reward signal. However, it is not tailored to mathematical reasoning,
and the resulting trends do not align as closely with empirical
practice. A primary reason is that it does not account for the
finite-sample regime, which, as we argue in this paper, is important to
understanding self-improvement in mathematical reasoning.

## 3 Problem Setup and Notation

##### Problem setup.

We introduce a theoretical formulation of a practical single-iteration
self-improvement procedure for mathematical
reasoning \[[42](#bib.bib28), [37](#bib.bib2), [16](#bib.bib3),
[21](#bib.bib5)\]. At iteration $`t`$ (with $`t`$ starting from $`0`$),
we sample questions $`q\sim p_{0}`$ and generate answers
$`a\sim\pi_{\theta_{t}}(\cdot\mid q)`$ using the current model
parameters $`\theta_{t}`$. Unless otherwise stated, we generate a single
candidate answer per question. For each pair $`(q,a)`$ we define a
reward (score) function $`s(q,a)\in[0,1]`$, where a larger reward
indicates a better answer. We retain only samples whose reward is at
least a threshold $`\tau\in(0,1]`$ and discard the rest.

At the population level, this filtering induces the distribution

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
D^{\prime}_{p_{0},\theta_{t}}(q,a)=\frac{p_{0}(q)\,\pi_{\theta_{t}}(a\mid q)\,\mathbf{1}_{\{\,s(q,a)\geq\tau\,\}}}{Z_{p_{0}}(\theta_{t})},
``` |  |

where
$`\alpha(\theta,q):=\Pr_{a\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}\!\big[s(q,a)\geq\tau\big]`$¹¹
1 Since an LLM can, in principle, assign nonzero probability to any
reasonable text continuation, including a correct solution, we treat
$`\alpha(\theta,q)>0`$ throughout. and
$`Z_{p}(\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p}\big[\alpha(\theta,q)\big]`$ denote
the per-question and global acceptance rates, respectively. The
idealized model update is then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\theta_{t+1}=\arg\max_{\theta}\;\mathbb{E}_{(q,a)\sim D^{\prime}_{p_{0},\theta_{t}}}\!\big[\log\pi_{\theta}(a\mid q)\big].
``` |  |

In practice, with finite samples, given the current model
$`\hat{\theta}_{t}`$ and a dataset of $`n`$ sampled questions, we obtain
a random number $`n_{t}`$ (with $`n_{t}\leq n`$) of accepted samples
$`\{(q_{i},a_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{t}}\sim D^{\prime}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}},`$
and perform empirical maximum likelihood estimation:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hat{\theta}_{t+1}=\arg\max_{\theta}\;\frac{1}{n_{t}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}}\log\pi_{\theta}(a_{i}\mid q_{i}).
``` |  |

Our goal is to relate this empirical self-improvement objective to the
evaluation metric, namely the expected reward under $`p_{0}`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t+1}):=\mathbb{E}_{(q,a)\sim D_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}}\!\big[s(q,a)\big],
``` |  |

where $`D_{p,\theta}(q,a):=p(q)\pi_{\theta}(a\mid q).`$

##### Notation.

Appendix Table [5](#A1.T5 "Table 5 ‣ A.1 Notation Summary ‣ A. Notation and Deferred Definitions ‣ Appendix: Proofs ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
summarizes the notation used throughout the paper, together with brief
descriptions and definitions.

## 4 Iterative Self-Improvement

This section develops basic theoretical tools for iterative
self-improvement. We start with a single-step analysis under a general
reward function $`s(q,a)`$ and threshold $`\tau`$. We then specialize to
mathematical reasoning and study the resulting multi-step
self-improvement, which will serve as a foundation for our analysis of
the easy-to-hard curriculum in
Section [5](#S5 "5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

### 4.1 Single-Step Self-Improvement

We begin by analyzing a single round of self-improvement in the
finite-sample regime.

###### Theorem 4.1.

Fix an iteration $`t`$ with current model $`\hat{\theta}_{t}`$. Let
$`\mathcal{Q}`$ denote the question space and let
$`\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$ be the set of probability measures on the answer
space $`\mathcal{A}`$. Let
$`\Pi\subset(\mathcal{Q}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}))`$ be a finite model
class, and suppose that the conditional distribution over answers
induced by $`D^{\prime}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}`$ belongs to $`\Pi`$.
Suppose that for each $`q\sim p_{0}`$ we draw $`m`$ ($`m\geq 1`$) i.i.d.
candidates
$`a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{m}\sim\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(\cdot\mid q)`$
sequentially and keep the first accepted candidate: we include
$`(q,a_{j})`$ in the training data where
$`j:=\min\{i\in[m]:s(q,a_{i})\geq\tau\}`$, and discard $`q`$ if no such
$`j`$ exists. Let $`n_{t}^{(m)}`$ be the resulting number of accepted
training pairs. Assume that
$`\operatorname{ess\,inf}_{q}\,\alpha(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)>0.`$ Then,
with probability at least $`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t+1})\;\geq\;\tau\left(1-\frac{Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})}{\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t})}\,\sqrt{\frac{2\log\!\big(|\Pi|\,\delta^{-1}\big)}{n_{t}^{\smash{(m)}}\,}}\right),
``` |  |

where
$`\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t},q):=1-\big(1-\alpha(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)\big)^{m},`$
$`Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t}):=\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p_{0}}\!\big[\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)\big],`$
and
$`\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t}):=\operatorname{ess\,inf}_{q}\,\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t},q).`$
Moreover, the ratio
$`Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})/\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t})`$ is
non-increasing in $`m`$ and satisfies
$`\lim_{m\to\infty}Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})/\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t})=1.`$

Theorem [4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ 4.1 Single-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
extends our setup in
Section [3](#S3 "3 Problem Setup and Notation ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula");
it reduces to the setting in
Section [3](#S3 "3 Problem Setup and Notation ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
by taking $`m=1`$, in which case
$`Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})=Z_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})`$ and
$`n_{t}^{(m)}=n_{t}`$.
Theorem [4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ 4.1 Single-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
highlight the importance of finite-sample effects for characterizing
self-improvement. Concretely, with infinite samples, the idealized
update yields $`\hat{\theta}_{t+1}`$ satisfying

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(a\mid q)=\frac{\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(a\mid q)\,\mathbf{1}_{\{\,s(q,a)\geq\tau\,\}}}{\alpha(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)},
``` |  |

and hence $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t+1})\geq\tau`$. In other words, an
infinite-sample (population) update would suggest that a single
iteration already guarantees performance above $`\tau`$ and that this
guarantee is independent of $`\hat{\theta}_{t}`$, both of which are
inconsistent with practice. This motivates our finite-sample regime
analysis, which further shows that (i) the ratio
$`Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})/\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t})`$
decreases with $`m`$, and (ii) the effective sample size $`n_{t}^{(m)}`$
increases (in expectation) with both $`n`$ and $`m`$. Consequently, to
obtain a stronger guarantee on self-improvement (i.e., a larger lower
bound on $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t+1})`$), it is beneficial to
increase both the question budget $`n`$ and the per-question answer
budget $`m`$.

###### Remark 4.2 (On the model class $`\Pi`$).

Our assumption of a finite model class $`\Pi`$ is consistent with prior
theoretical treatments of self-improvement \[[17](#bib.bib9)\]. More
importantly, the effectiveness of recent work in formalizing
self-improvement as tree search over a finite archive of candidate
agents \[[33](#bib.bib29)\] suggests that the candidate set $`|\Pi|`$ is
typically not very large in practice. Moreover, evidence that stronger
language models admit a smaller intrinsic dimension during
fine-tuning \[[1](#bib.bib30)\] indicates that $`|\Pi|`$ tends to be
smaller for stronger base models.

### 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement

We specialize to mathematical reasoning by adopting a binary reward
$`s(q,a)\in\{0,1\}`$, where $`s(q,a)=1`$ indicates a correct
(verifiable) solution and $`s(q,a)=0`$ otherwise. In this regime,
combined with
Assumption [4.3](#S4.Thmtheorem3 "Assumption 4.3. ‣ 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
which posits a positive relationship between the per-question acceptance
rate and the expected reward for all but a $`\gamma`$ fraction of
questions,
Corollary [4.4](#S4.Thmtheorem4 "Corollary 4.4. ‣ 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
relates $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t+1})`$ directly to the pretrained
initialization performance $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})`$ through an
iterated map.

###### Assumption 4.3.

Let $`\Theta`$ be a small neighborhood of the pretrained initialization
in which post-training is performed. Then there exist a constant
$`c\in(0,1)`$ and a small constant $`\gamma\geq 0`$ such that for any
question distribution $`p`$ and model $`\theta\in\Theta`$,
$`\Pr_{q\sim p}\!\big[\alpha(\theta,q)<c\,V_{p}(\theta)\big]\leq\gamma.`$

###### Corollary 4.4.

Consider the binary reward setting $`s(q,a)\in\{0,1\}`$, where each
iteration $`t`$ uses the same question budget $`n`$ with $`n_{t}\leq n`$
accepted samples. Under
Assumption [4.3](#S4.Thmtheorem3 "Assumption 4.3. ‣ 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
F(x)\;:=\;1-\gamma-\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{x-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}}
``` |  |

on its natural domain $`x>c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu`$, where
$`\nu:=\sqrt{1/n}`$, $`c_{\delta}:=\sqrt{2\log(|\Pi|\,\delta^{-1})}`$,
and $`c_{\delta^{\prime}}:=\sqrt{\log(\delta^{\prime-1})/2}`$. Then,
with probability at least $`1-\delta-\delta^{\prime}`$,
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t+1})\;\geq\;F\big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})\big).`$
Moreover, with probability at least $`1-t(\delta+\delta^{\prime})`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})\geq F^{\circ t}\big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big),
``` |  |

where $`F^{\circ t}`$ denotes the $`t`$-fold composition of $`F`$.

###### Proposition 4.5.

Under the setting of
Corollary [4.4](#S4.Thmtheorem4 "Corollary 4.4. ‣ 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
let $`\nu`$ be sufficiently small such that

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
0<\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\,(1-\gamma-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu)^{3/2}}<\frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}}.
``` |  |

Let
$`\mathcal{I}(1,\nu)=(x_{-}(1,\nu),x_{+}(1,\nu))\subset(c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu,\,1-\gamma)`$
be the interval defined in
Definition [A.1](#A1.Thmtheorem1 "Definition A.1 (ℐ(𝑎,𝜈)). ‣ A.2 Deferred Definitions and Explicit Expressions ‣ A. Notation and Deferred Definitions ‣ Appendix: Proofs ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
with $`a=1`$. Then, for any non-negative integer $`t`$,
$`F^{\circ(t+1)}\big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)>F^{\circ t}\big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)`$
and
$`F^{\circ t}\big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)\in\mathcal{I}(1,\nu)`$
hold if and only if
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\in\mathcal{I}(1,\nu)`$. Moreover,
$`x_{-}(1,\nu)`$ is increasing in $`\nu`$, $`x_{+}(1,\nu)`$ is
decreasing in $`\nu`$, and the interval length
$`|\mathcal{I}(1,\nu)|=x_{+}(1,\nu)-x_{-}(1,\nu)`$ is decreasing in
$`\nu`$ and satisfies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|\mathcal{I}(1,\nu)|\;\geq\;(1-\gamma-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu)-\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}\cdot\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{\,1-\gamma-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu\,}}.
``` |  |

Remark 4.6.1 (Moderate task difficulty benefits iterative
self-improvement).
Corollary [4.4](#S4.Thmtheorem4 "Corollary 4.4. ‣ 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
and
Proposition [4.5](#S4.Thmtheorem5 "Proposition 4.5. ‣ 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
suggest that iterative self-improvement admits monotonic lower-bound
guarantees only when the task difficulty is neither too hard nor too
easy for the pretrained initialization such that
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\in\mathcal{I}(1,\nu)`$. Within
$`\mathcal{I}(1,\nu)`$, better models admit more data per iteration
being accepted, thereby sustaining self-improvement over successive
iterations.

Remark 4.6.2 (Benefits of larger budgets). Increasing the question
budget $`n`$ (i.e., decreasing $`\nu`$) enlarges the interval
$`\mathcal{I}(1,\nu)`$, and hence enlarges the set of initial
performances for which the bound sequence
$`\{F^{\circ t}(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0}))\}_{t\geq 0}`$ is guaranteed
to be strictly increasing.

Remark 4.6.3 (Inherent upper bound). Iterative self-improvement is
inherently bounded: for
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\in\mathcal{I}(1,\nu)`$, the lower bound
cannot exceed $`x_{+}(1,\nu)`$, which is strictly below $`1-\gamma`$.
This provides a rationale for practical mathematical reasoning pipelines
to incorporate additional optimization phases (e.g., reinforcement
learning \[[16](#bib.bib3)\]) to push performance further.

## 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement

Combining self-improvement with an easy-to-hard curriculum across
iterations has emerged as a promising approach for further improving
model performance by progressively increasing the difficulty of
questions encountered in different rounds \[[25](#bib.bib6),
[18](#bib.bib24), [19](#bib.bib25)\]. Despite its empirical appeal, a
principled theoretical understanding of such curriculum-guided
self-improvement remains limited. In this section, we extend the tools
developed in
Section [4](#S4 "4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
to study when and why integrating iterative self-improvement with an
easy-to-hard curriculum can yield stronger self-improvement guarantees.

### 5.1 Easy-to-Hard Curriculum and Baseline

##### Difficulty levels.

We assume there exist $`L`$ ($`L\geq 2`$) task distributions
$`p_{1},\ldots,p_{L}`$, where each $`p_{i}`$ is a valid question
distribution, and the difficulty increases progressively from $`p_{1}`$
to $`p_{L}`$.
Assumption [5.1](#S5.Thmtheorem1 "Assumption 5.1. ‣ Difficulty levels. ‣ 5.1 Easy-to-Hard Curriculum and Baseline ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
formalizes this notion via a power-law separation between adjacent tasks
(in difficulty) $`p_{i}`$ and $`p_{i+1}`$. Concretely, under
Assumption [5.1](#S5.Thmtheorem1 "Assumption 5.1. ‣ Difficulty levels. ‣ 5.1 Easy-to-Hard Curriculum and Baseline ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
for every $`i\in[L-1]`$ and every $`\theta\in\Theta`$, we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
1\;<\;\frac{i^{-\beta^{\prime}}}{(i+1)^{-\beta^{\prime}}}\;\leq\;\frac{V_{p_{i}}(\theta)}{V_{p_{i+1}}(\theta)}\;\leq\;\frac{i^{-\beta}}{(i+1)^{-\beta}}.
``` |  |

This reflects the view that expected reward is a natural measure of task
difficulty, and that the relative ordering of difficulty levels should
be model-invariant for $`\theta\in\Theta`$. Moreover, a larger
$`\beta^{\prime}`$ corresponds to a larger difficulty ratio between
adjacent tasks, while a larger uncertainty width
$`\Delta:=\beta-\beta^{\prime}`$ indicates greater ambiguity in this
difficulty ratio.

###### Assumption 5.1.

Let $`\Theta`$ be a small neighborhood of the pretrained initialization
in which post-training is performed. Consider $`L`$ question
distributions $`\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{L}\}`$. For $`\theta\in\Theta`$
and $`i\in[L]`$, define the expected reward
$`V_{p_{i}}(\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{(q,a)\sim D_{p_{i},\theta}}\!\big[s(q,a)\big].`$
Define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\beta^{\prime}\coloneqq\min_{i\in[L-1]}\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{\log\!\big(V_{p_{i}}(\theta)/V_{p_{i+1}}(\theta)\big)}{\log(1+1/i)}\quad\text{and}\quad\beta\coloneqq\max_{i\in[L-1]}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{\log\!\big(V_{p_{i}}(\theta)/V_{p_{i+1}}(\theta)\big)}{\log(1+1/i)}.
``` |  |

We assume that $`0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta`$.

##### Easy-to-hard.

For the easy-to-hard curriculum, we consider $`L`$ iterations of
self-improvement. At each iteration $`t\in\{0,1,\ldots,L-1\}`$, we
sample $`n`$ questions from $`p_{t+1}`$ to reflect progressively
increasing difficulty, and perform one round of self-improvement using
the current model. We initialize the curriculum with
$`\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{E2H}}_{0}=\hat{\theta}_{0}`$, and denote the
model after iteration $`t`$ by $`\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{E2H}}_{t+1}`$.

##### Baseline.

The baseline we compare against trains for $`L`$ iterations using a
fixed and uniform mixture over all difficulty levels. At each iteration
$`t\in\{0,1,\ldots,L-1\}`$, we always sample $`n`$ training questions
from $`p_{0}:=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}p_{i}.`$ We use the same
initialization $`\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{B}}_{0}=\hat{\theta}_{0}`$, and
denote the model after iteration $`t`$ by
$`\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{B}}_{t+1}`$.

### 5.2 Main Results

We now present our core comparison between the final self-improvement
performance under the baseline,
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{B}}_{L})`$, and under the easy-to-hard
curriculum, $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{E2H}}_{L})`$.
Theorem [5.2](#S5.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 5.2. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
provides feasibility conditions that characterize when the lower bound
sequences for both training schemes are monotone across iterations, and
an improvement condition under which the easy-to-hard curriculum yields
a strictly tighter lower bound than the baseline. These sufficient
conditions are highly predictive in practice: they closely track the
trends observed in our Monte-Carlo simulations in this section and are
consistent with the empirical gains on mathematical reasoning tasks
reported in
Section [6](#S6 "6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

###### Theorem 5.2.

Follow the notation of
Corollary [4.4](#S4.Thmtheorem4 "Corollary 4.4. ‣ 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").
Fix all parameters except $`\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu`$ and
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})`$.

(i) Suppose feasibility conditions
$`\mathcal{M}_{i}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)<0`$
holds²² 2 We defer the explicit forms of
$`\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}`$ and $`\mathcal{N}`$ to
Definition [A.2](#A1.Thmtheorem2 "Definition A.2 ({ℳ_𝑖}_{𝑖=1}^4 and 𝒩). ‣ A.2 Deferred Definitions and Explicit Expressions ‣ A. Notation and Deferred Definitions ‣ Appendix: Proofs ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").
for all $`i\in[4]`$. Then, w.h.p., the following statements hold. For
the baseline,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{p_{0}}\!\big(\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{B}}_{L}\big)\;\geq\;F^{\circ L}\Big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\Big),
``` |  |

and the sequence
$`\{F^{\circ t}(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0}))\}_{t\geq 0}`$ is
monotonically increasing in $`t`$. For the easy-to-hard curriculum,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{p_{0}}\!\big(\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{E2H}}_{L}\big)\;\geq\;(G\circ H_{L-1}\circ H_{L-2}\circ\cdots\circ H_{0})\Big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\Big),
``` |  |

where for each $`t\in\{0,1,\ldots,L-1\}`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
H_{t}(x)\;:=\;1-\gamma-\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{a_{t}x-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}},\qquad G(x):=a_{L}x,
``` |  |

and $`a_{0}=L/\sum_{i=1}^{L}i^{-\beta^{\prime}}`$,
$`a_{L}=\sum_{i=1}^{L}i^{-\beta^{\prime}}/L^{1-\beta^{\prime}}`$,
$`a_{t}=(t+1)^{-\beta}/t^{-\beta}`$ for $`t\in[L-1]`$. Also, the
sequence
$`\{(H_{t}\circ\cdots\circ H_{0})\big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)\}_{t\geq 0}`$
is monotonically increasing in $`t`$.

(ii) If the improvement condition
$`\mathcal{N}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)<0`$
further holds^(†)^(†)footnotemark: , then the easy-to-hard lower bound
is strictly larger than the baseline lower bound:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
(G\circ H_{L-1}\circ H_{L-2}\circ\cdots\circ H_{0})\Big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\Big)\;>\;F^{\circ L}\Big(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\Big).
``` |  |

##### Interpreting $`\{\mathcal{M}_{i}<0\}`$.

To enable meaningful comparisons across iterations, feasibility
conditions $`\{\mathcal{M}_{i}<0\}_{i=1}^{4}`$ in
Theorem [5.2](#S5.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 5.2. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
rule out degenerate regimes in which the evolution of the expected
reward lower bound become ill-defined or fail to be monotonically
increasing. We provide a concrete interpretation of the region
$`\{\mathcal{M}_{i}<0\}_{i=1}^{4}`$ in
Remark [5.4](#S5.Thmtheorem4 "Remark 5.4 (Feasibility disfavors small budgets and large adjacent difficulty ratios). ‣ Interpreting {ℳ_𝑖<0}. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

###### Corollary 5.3.

Follow the setting of
Theorem [5.2](#S5.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 5.2. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").
Let
$`\mathcal{I}(2^{-\beta},\nu)=(x_{-}(2^{-\beta},\nu),x_{+}(2^{-\beta},\nu))`$
be the interval in
Definition [A.1](#A1.Thmtheorem1 "Definition A.1 (ℐ(𝑎,𝜈)). ‣ A.2 Deferred Definitions and Explicit Expressions ‣ A. Notation and Deferred Definitions ‣ Appendix: Proofs ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
with $`a=2^{-\beta}`$. Then the feasibility conditions
$`\mathcal{M}_{i}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)<0`$
for all $`i\in[4]`$ are equivalent to
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)`$,
where

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu):=\Big(x_{-}(2^{-\beta},\nu),\,\frac{2^{-\beta}}{a_{0}}x_{+}(2^{-\beta},\nu)\Big).
``` |  |

Moreover, the interval length
$`|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)|`$ satisfies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle 2^{\beta}c_{\delta^{\prime}}\,\nu\;\leq\;\big|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,0)\big|-\big|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)\big|\;\leq\;2^{\beta}c_{\delta^{\prime}}\,\nu+\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}\cdot\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{\,2^{-\beta}(1-\gamma)-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu\,}}.`$ |  |

###### Remark 5.4 (Feasibility disfavors small budgets and large adjacent difficulty ratios).

Corollaries [5.3](#S5.Thmtheorem3 "Corollary 5.3. ‣ Interpreting {ℳ_𝑖<0}. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
and [B.3](#A2.Thmtheorem3 "Corollary B.3. ‣ B.3 Proof of Proposition ‣ B. Proofs for Section ‣ Appendix: Proofs ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
together imply that
$`|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)|`$ decreases in
$`\nu`$, $`\beta^{\prime}`$, and $`\beta`$, and that its shrinkage rate
in $`\nu`$ is $`\Theta(\nu)`$ as $`\nu\to 0`$. Although
$`\{\mathcal{M}_{i}<0\}_{i=1}^{4}`$ only enforces feasibility (rather
than directly characterizing when the easy-to-hard curriculum improves
over the baseline), we generally prefer
$`|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)|`$ not to be too
small. Consequently, (i) an overly small question budget $`n`$ and (ii)
overly large difficulty ratios between adjacent tasks are both
undesirable from the standpoint of feasibility. Finally,
Figure [1](#S5.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ Interpreting {ℳ_𝑖<0}. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
shows that the condition
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)`$
closely matches the behavior observed in direct Monte-Carlo simulations,
making $`\{\mathcal{M}_{i}<0\}_{i=1}^{4}`$ a useful proxy.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
| ![Refer to caption](2602.10014v3/e2h_feasible_sim_mc_fixbetaprime_varybeta_k.png) (a) | ![Refer to caption](2602.10014v3/e2h_feasible_sim_cond_fixbetaprime_varybeta_k.png) (b) | ![Refer to caption](2602.10014v3/e2h_feasible_sim_mc_fixbeta_varybetaprime_k.png) (c) | ![Refer to caption](2602.10014v3/e2h_feasible_sim_cond_fixbeta_varybetaprime_k.png) (d) |

$`\{(H_{t}\circ\cdots\circ H_{0})(V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0}))\}_{t\geq 0}`$
are both monotonically increasing in $`t`$, under different

the feasibility interval
$`\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)`$ in
Corollary [5.3](#S5.Thmtheorem3 "Corollary 5.3. ‣ Interpreting {ℳ_𝑖<0}. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

holds under different $`(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)`$ settings. Panel

$`\Delta=0.1`$ and vary $`(\beta^{\prime},\nu)`$; the same panel also
includes a zoomed-in view for small $`\beta^{\prime}`$.

##### Interpreting $`\mathcal{N}<0`$.

$`\mathcal{N}<0`$ serves as the key criterion for improvement: it
guarantees that the easy-to-hard curriculum attains a strictly larger
final lower bound than the baseline. A concrete interpretation of
$`\mathcal{N}<0`$ is provided in
Remarks [5.2](#S5.SS2.SSS0.Px2 "Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")–[5.2](#S5.SS2.SSS0.Px2 "Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").
Notably, all the resulting predictions based on the improvement
condition $`\mathcal{N}<0`$ closely match the trends observed in direct
Monte-Carlo simulations in
Figure [2](#S5.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Interpreting {ℳ_𝑖<0}. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

###### Proposition 5.5.

Follow the setting of
Theorem [5.2](#S5.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 5.2. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").
Then the improvement condition
$`\mathcal{N}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)<0`$
is equivalent to
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu),`$
where

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)\;:=\;\bigl(x(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu),\,1-\gamma\bigr).
``` |  |

For fixed $`(\beta^{\prime},\beta)`$, we write
$`x(\nu)\coloneqq x(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)`$ for brevity. Then,
$`x(\nu)`$ is monotonically increasing in $`\nu`$ and satisfies
$`x(0)=0`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
x^{\prime}(\nu)=\frac{c_{\delta^{\prime}}}{a_{0}}+\frac{2}{a_{0}}\Big(\frac{c_{\delta}}{c(1-\gamma)}\Big)^{\!2}\nu+O\big(\nu^{5/3}\big)\quad\text{as }\nu\to 0.
``` |  |

Moreover, there exists a unique critical value $`\nu_{c}>0`$ and a
constant $`C(\nu_{c})>0`$ such that

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
x^{\prime}(\nu)\;=\;\frac{C(\nu_{c})}{(\nu_{c}-\nu)^{3}}\,\bigl(1+O(\nu_{c}-\nu)\bigr)\quad\text{as }\nu\uparrow\nu_{c}.
``` |  |

###### Corollary 5.6 (Informal version; formal version stated in Corollary [C.4](#A3.Thmtheorem4 "Corollary C.4 (Formal version of Corollary ). ‣ C.4 Proof of Corollary ‣ C. Proofs for Section ‣ Appendix: Proofs ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")).

In
Proposition [5.5](#S5.Thmtheorem5 "Proposition 5.5. ‣ Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
fix any initialization $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\in(0,1-\gamma)`$,
and let $`\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta)`$ be defined by the
threshold equation
$`x\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta)\big)=V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0}).`$
Then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta)\;=\;\sup\big\{\,\nu>0:\ \mathcal{N}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)<0\,\big\}.
``` |  |

Moreover, (i) fixing $`\beta^{\prime}`$, for $`\beta>\beta^{\prime}`$,
$`\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta)`$ is decreasing in $`\beta`$;

(ii) fixing $`\beta`$, for $`\beta^{\prime}\in(0,\beta)`$,
$`\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta)`$ is increasing in
$`\beta^{\prime}`$;

(iii) fixing $`\Delta=\beta-\beta^{\prime}`$ and writing
$`\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta)`$ as
$`\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\Delta)`$, when
$`\beta^{\prime}`$ is sufficiently small,
$`\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\Delta)`$ is increasing in
$`\beta^{\prime}`$ and scales as $`\Theta(\beta^{\prime})`$; when
$`\beta^{\prime}`$ is sufficiently large,
$`\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\Delta)\to 0`$, with the
tail bound $`O(2^{-\beta^{\prime}})`$. Furthermore, for any finite range
$`[0,B]`$ whose endpoint $`B`$ is above an explicit computable
threshold, in the large-sample regime,
$`\nu^{\star}(\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}+\Delta)`$ is first
increasing and then decreasing in $`\beta^{\prime}`$, with a unique
optimizer on this range.

Remark 5.7.1 (Phase transition with respect to the question budget).
Proposition [5.5](#S5.Thmtheorem5 "Proposition 5.5. ‣ Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
shows that the interval length
$`|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)|`$ decreases as
$`\nu`$ increases. Moreover, the shrinkage rate is mild when $`\nu`$ is
small (since $`c_{\delta^{\prime}}`$ is typically small and
$`a_{0}>1`$), but becomes steep as $`\nu`$ approaches the critical value
$`\nu_{c}`$ ($`x^{\prime}(\nu)`$ blows up on the order of
$`\Theta((\nu_{c}-\nu)^{-3})`$. Equivalently, decreasing the question
budget $`n`$ makes it harder for the easy-to-hard curriculum to provably
outperform the baseline, and there is a critical sample size such that
as $`n`$ decreases toward this threshold, the range of initializations
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})`$ for which easy-to-hard is provably
advantageous collapses sharply.

Remark 5.7.2 (Smaller uncertainty in the adjacent difficulty ratio is
better). Parts (i)-(ii) of
Corollary [5.6](#S5.Thmtheorem6 "Corollary 5.6 (Informal version; formal version stated in Corollary ). ‣ Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
imply that, whether we fix $`\beta^{\prime}`$ or $`\beta`$, as the
uncertainty width $`\Delta=\beta-\beta^{\prime}`$ increases, the maximal
admissible $`\nu`$ (and hence the minimal question budget $`n`$) for
$`\mathcal{N}<0`$ becomes more stringent.

Remark 5.7.3 (Moderate difficulty ratios between adjacent tasks are most
favorable). Part (iii) of
Corollary [5.6](#S5.Thmtheorem6 "Corollary 5.6 (Informal version; formal version stated in Corollary ). ‣ Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
further shows that, when $`\Delta`$ is fixed, as the difficulty ratios
between adjacent tasks (captured by $`\beta^{\prime}`$) increase, the
minimal admissible sample budget $`n`$ required for the easy-to-hard
curriculum to be provably better than the baseline decreases in the
small-ratio regime, but diverges to infinity once the difficulty ratios
between adjacent tasks become sufficiently large. Moreover, over any
sufficiently large finite interval of $`\beta^{\prime}`$, in the
large-sample regime, there exists a unique optimal $`\beta^{\prime}`$
that minimizes the required sample budget.

Remark 5.7.4 (Improvement dominates for small budgets, while feasibility
dominates for large budgets). By
Corollary [5.3](#S5.Thmtheorem3 "Corollary 5.3. ‣ Interpreting {ℳ_𝑖<0}. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
and
Proposition [5.5](#S5.Thmtheorem5 "Proposition 5.5. ‣ Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
for any fixed $`(\beta^{\prime},\beta)`$, both
$`|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)|`$ and
$`|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{N}}(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu)|`$ decrease as
$`\nu`$ increases. Moreover, since
$`c_{\delta^{\prime}}/a_{0}<2^{\beta}c_{\delta^{\prime}}`$, we have the
following dichotomy: as $`\nu\to 0`$, the shrinkage of the admissible
range of $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})`$ is dominated by the feasibility
conditions $`\{\mathcal{M}_{i}<0\}_{i=1}^{4}`$; whereas as $`\nu`$
approaches $`\nu_{c}`$, the shrinkage is dominated by the improvement
condition $`\mathcal{N}<0`$.

## 6 Experiment

In this section, we empirically validate the main theoretical
predictions developed in
Sections [4](#S4 "4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
and [5](#S5 "5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
across diverse mathematical reasoning tasks. We begin with a synthetic
shortest path task, which enables precise control over key variables in
our theory. We then show that the resulting trends are also consistent
with those observed on different standard mathematical reasoning
benchmarks.

### 6.1 Experiments on Synthetic Tasks

#### 6.1.1 Experimental Setup

##### Shortest path.

Given the capability of LLMs to solve graph problems in natural
language \[[32](#bib.bib26)\], we study self-improvement on a shortest
path task using synthetically generated graphs. We consider a directed
unweighted graph $`\mathcal{G}`$. Our task is: given $`\mathcal{G}`$ and
two distinct vertices $`v_{s}\neq v_{t}`$ in $`\mathcal{G}`$, predict
the shortest path length $`l`$, i.e., the minimum number of edges among
all directed paths from $`v_{s}`$ to $`v_{t}`$; if no such path exists,
we set $`l=-1`$.

##### Datasets and model training setup.

Across experiments, we construct synthetic datasets from a sample pool
consisting of a large collection of distinct graphs $`\mathcal{G}`$
together with vertex pairs $`(v_{s},v_{t})`$, spanning different choices
of the number of nodes $`N`$, expected out-degree $`\bar{d}`$, and
target distance $`l`$. This synthetic shortest path task enables direct
control of several key variables, including the initialization expected
reward $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})`$, sample budgets $`n`$ and $`m`$,
and difficulty ratios between adjacent tasks. We use
Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct as our base LLM \[[14](#bib.bib27)\]. We follow
the procedure described in
Section [3](#S3 "3 Problem Setup and Notation ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
and the easy-to-hard/baseline setup in
Section [5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Easy-to-Hard Curriculum and Baseline ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
to run iterative self-improvement. More details of dataset construction,
parameter control, and self-improvement finetuning are provided in
Appendix [D.1](#Ax2.SSx1.SSSx1 "D.1 Experiments on Synthetic Tasks ‣ D. Implementation Details and Training Setup ‣ Appendix: Experimental Details and Additional Discussions ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

##### Evaluation metric.

In the binary reward setting, the expected reward is equivalent to the
population Pass@1. Therefore, we report the Pass@1 accuracy on a
held-out test set sampled from $`p_{0}`$ as our evaluation metric.

#### 6.1.2 Experimental Results

|     |     |     |
|-----|-----|-----|
| (a) | (b) | (c) |

Figure 3: Iterative self-improvement results on the synthetic shortest
path task. Panel (a) shows the self-improvement trajectories of a fixed
$`\hat{\theta}_{0}`$ across tasks with different initial Pass@1
accuracies; hollow markers and faded line segments indicate model
collapse (Pass@1$`=0`$ for at least one target distance $`l`$). Panel
(b) shows the performance under different question budgets $`n`$, with
$`\hat{\theta}_{0}`$ and the initial Pass@1 fixed. Panel (c) shows the
performance under different per-question answer budgets $`m`$, with
$`\hat{\theta}_{0}`$ and the initial Pass@1 fixed.

##### Iterative self-improvement.

Figure [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1.2 Experimental Results ‣ 6.1 Experiments on Synthetic Tasks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")(a)
shows the iterative self-improvement performance under tasks of varying
difficulty, where we construct task sets such that the initial Pass@1
accuracy (corresponding to $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})`$) ranges from
$`6\%`$ to $`78\%`$. When the task is overly easy, we observe a
decreasing trend in the test Pass@1 across iterations, whereas when the
task is overly hard, the Pass@1 becomes unstable and may collapse. These
trends are consistent with the analysis in
Remark [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
which predicts that effective iterative self-improvement is only
guaranteed to occur in a moderate difficulty regime. We also note that
the improvement in Pass@1 often slows down after $`t=2`$ and the curves
begin to plateau at values clearly below $`1`$, which is consistent with
Remark [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").
Moreover,
Figure [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1.2 Experimental Results ‣ 6.1 Experiments on Synthetic Tasks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")(b)-(c)
demonstrate that increasing either the question budget $`n`$ or the
per-question answer budget $`m`$ consistently improves self-improvement
performance. This aligns with the finite-sample interpretation in
Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Single-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

|-----|-----|
| (a) | (b) |

Figure 4: Iterative self-improvement with easy-to-hard curriculum on the
synthetic shortest path task. Panel (a) fixes $`\Delta=0.04`$ and
$`\hat{\theta}_{0}`$, and shows for different initial Pass@1 accuracies,
the final Pass@1 gap between easy-to-hard and the baseline (i.e.,
$`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{E2H}}_{L})-V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{B}}_{L})`$)
as a function of the adjacent task difficulty ratio (captured by
$`\beta^{\prime}`$). Panel (b) fixes $`\Delta=0.04`$ and
$`\beta^{\prime}=0.25`$, and shows for different initial Pass@1
accuracies (spanning $`35\%`$–$`55\%`$), how the final Pass@1 gap varies
with the question budget $`n`$. The solid line reports the mean gap
across 15 initializations and the shaded region indicates $`\pm 1`$
standard error; the gray bars (right axis) show the number of
initializations with a positive gap at each $`n`$.

##### Iterative self-improvement with easy-to-hard curriculum.

In
Figure [4](#S6.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Iterative self-improvement. ‣ 6.1.2 Experimental Results ‣ 6.1 Experiments on Synthetic Tasks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")(a),
each curve varies the difficulty ratios between adjacent tasks
(controlled by $`\beta^{\prime}`$) while keeping the initial Pass@1
(corresponding to $`V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})`$) fixed. Across
different values of the initial Pass@1, the final Pass@1 gap between
easy-to-hard and the baseline exhibits an overall trend of first
increasing and then decreasing as $`\beta^{\prime}`$ grows, with the
largest gaps typically attained around $`\beta^{\prime}\in[0.2,0.5]`$.
This aligns with the discussion in
Remark [5.2](#S5.SS2.SSS0.Px2 "Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
which suggests that moderate difficulty ratios between adjacent tasks
are desirable.

Figure [4](#S6.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Iterative self-improvement. ‣ 6.1.2 Experimental Results ‣ 6.1 Experiments on Synthetic Tasks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")(b)
reports results under different initial Pass@1 accuracies, with a fixed
relative difficulty across tasks (i.e., fixed $`\beta^{\prime}`$ and
$`\Delta`$). First, we observe that larger question budgets $`n`$ lead
to a larger final Pass@1 gap of easy-to-hard over the baseline on
average; moreover, as $`n`$ increases, the final Pass@1 gap varies less
across different initial performances, leading to a smaller standard
error (the number of initializations is fixed). Second, for a diverse
set of initial Pass@1 accuracies in the range of $`35\%`$–$`55\%`$, the
question budget $`n`$ at which easy-to-hard starts to outperform the
baseline mostly clusters in a relatively narrow range, roughly between
$`2000`$ and $`3000`$. This agrees with the phase transition behavior
predicted in
Remark [5.2](#S5.SS2.SSS0.Px2 "Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

### 6.2 Experiments on Standard Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks

#### 6.2.1 Experimental Setup

##### Datasets and model training setup.

We further validate our theoretical analysis on standard mathematical
reasoning benchmarks, including GSM8K \[[4](#bib.bib41)\] and DeepMind
Mathematics \[[26](#bib.bib40)\]. GSM8K contains 8.5K grade-school-level
math word problems. DeepMind Mathematics consists of mathematical
reasoning problems spanning multiple categories (e.g., algebra,
arithmetic, and numbers) with each category further containing a large
collection of fine-grained modules. Notably, DeepMind Mathematics comes
with a native easy/medium/hard difficulty partition, which naturally
supports our modeling of the easy-to-hard curriculum. Unless otherwise
specified, we use Qwen3-8B \[[38](#bib.bib43)\] as the base model
throughout
Section [6.2](#S6.SS2 "6.2 Experiments on Standard Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").
Additional base models considered in this section include
Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct, Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct \[[14](#bib.bib27)\],
Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct \[[39](#bib.bib42)\], and
Qwen3-32B \[[38](#bib.bib43)\]. As in
Section [6.1](#S6.SS1 "6.1 Experiments on Synthetic Tasks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
we follow the setup in
Section [3](#S3 "3 Problem Setup and Notation ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
and
Section [5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Easy-to-Hard Curriculum and Baseline ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
to run iterative self-improvement, and use Pass@1 accuracy as the
evaluation metric. More details on dataset splits and self-improvement
finetuning are provided in
Appendix [D.2](#Ax2.SSx1.SSSx2 "D.2 Experiments on Standard Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks ‣ D. Implementation Details and Training Setup ‣ Appendix: Experimental Details and Additional Discussions ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

#### 6.2.2 Experimental Results

##### Iterative self-improvement.

Table [1](#S6.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Datasets and model training setup. ‣ 6.2.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 6.2 Experiments on Standard Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
and
Table [2](#S6.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Datasets and model training setup. ‣ 6.2.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 6.2 Experiments on Standard Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
report the self-improvement trajectories on GSM8K and on the easy split
of the nine DeepMind Mathematics modules with the highest initial
Qwen3-8B accuracy, respectively. Across different base models, these
trajectories clearly show that when a task is too easy or too hard
relative to the model, self-improvement tends to be limited or even
deteriorate, whereas moderate task difficulty is more conducive to
sustained iterative self-improvement. Moreover, as the number of
iterations increases, the incremental improvement from self-improvement
tends to decrease.
Table [3](#S6.T3 "Table 3 ‣ Datasets and model training setup. ‣ 6.2.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 6.2 Experiments on Standard Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
further shows how the degree of self-improvement changes with the
question budget $`n`$ and the per-question answer budget $`m`$. The
results indicate that larger values of $`n`$ and $`m`$ are both more
favorable to self-improvement.

Overall, these findings are consistent with the synthetic task results
in
Section [6.1.2](#S6.SS1.SSS2 "6.1.2 Experimental Results ‣ 6.1 Experiments on Synthetic Tasks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
and provide empirical support for
Remark [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
Remark [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
and the finite-sample analysis in
Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Single-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").
It is worth noting that, despite minor differences in self-improvement
setups, a range of empirical studies further echo our findings on a
broader set of real-world benchmarks: self-improvement tends to favor a
moderate task difficulty regime \[[28](#bib.bib34)\], benefits from
larger budgets ($`n`$ \[[28](#bib.bib34), [34](#bib.bib35)\] and
$`m`$ \[[43](#bib.bib36), [2](#bib.bib38), [41](#bib.bib37)\]), and
often exhibits a saturation limit \[[29](#bib.bib11)\].

DeepMind Mathematics Dataset using Qwen3-8B. Difficulty follows the
dataset’s native easy/medium/hard split. $`\rho`$ interpolates between
fixed-mixture training in every round ($`\rho{=}0`$, baseline) and a
fully staged curriculum ($`\rho{=}1`$: iteration 0 easy, iteration 1
medium, iteration 2 hard). We run experiments on all modules in the
algebra, arithmetic, and numbers categories. To focus on modules for
which curriculum effects are meaningfully measurable, we exclude modules
whose canonical difficulty split yields $`\beta^{\prime}<0.1`$, or whose
gap variation across $`\rho`$ is smaller than 1%. Underlined entries
denote, for each module, the maximum gap across $`\rho`$. All values are
percentages. Standard errors are shown as subscripts.

Figure 5: The left panel plots the estimate of $`\beta^{\prime}`$ as a
function of $`\rho`$, and the right panel plots the estimate of
$`\beta`$ as a function of $`\rho`$, for all modules reported in
Table [4](#S6.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Iterative self-improvement. ‣ 6.2.2 Experimental Results ‣ 6.2 Experiments on Standard Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").
Here, the estimates of $`\beta^{\prime}`$ and $`\beta`$ are computed
from the initialization model using the form in
Assumption [5.1](#S5.Thmtheorem1 "Assumption 5.1. ‣ Difficulty levels. ‣ 5.1 Easy-to-Hard Curriculum and Baseline ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula").

##### Iterative self-improvement with easy-to-hard curriculum.

Beyond the synthetic experiments, here we further validate our analysis
using the dataset’s native difficulty partition. Concretely, for the
DeepMind Mathematics dataset, we vary the difficulty ratios between
adjacent tasks through a parameter $`\rho`$. For the $`L=3`$ iteration
self-improvement setting, let
$`\mathbf{W}=(w_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}`$, where $`w_{ij}`$
denotes the sampling weight assigned at iteration $`i-1`$ to the
$`j`$-th difficulty level, with $`j=1,2,3`$ corresponding to the easy,
medium, and hard splits, respectively. Let $`\mathbf{U}`$ be the matrix
whose entries are all $`1/3`$, and let $`\mathbf{I}`$ be the identity
matrix. We define

|     |                                                                  |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathbf{W}=(1-\rho)\mathbf{U}+\rho\mathbf{I},\qquad\rho\in[0,1].
       ```                                                               |     |

Thus, when $`\rho=0`$, we have $`\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{U}`$, which
corresponds to the baseline where each iteration samples uniformly from
all three difficulty levels; when $`\rho=1`$, we have
$`\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{I}`$, which corresponds to a fully staged
easy-to-hard curriculum in which each iteration samples exclusively from
one difficulty level. As $`\rho`$ increases from $`0`$ to $`1`$, the
difficulty ratios between adjacent tasks become larger.

Figure [5](#S6.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ Iterative self-improvement. ‣ 6.2.2 Experimental Results ‣ 6.2 Experiments on Standard Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
shows that the estimates of $`\beta`$ and $`\beta^{\prime}`$ both
increase clearly with $`\rho`$, suggesting that these $`\beta`$ and
$`\beta^{\prime}`$ provide effective proxies for the dataset’s native
notion of difficulty separation.
Table [4](#S6.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Iterative self-improvement. ‣ 6.2.2 Experimental Results ‣ 6.2 Experiments on Standard Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks ‣ 6 Experiment ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
further reports, for modules in the algebra, arithmetic, and numbers
categories, the final Pass@1 gap between easy-to-hard and the baseline
as a function of $`\rho`$. Across modules, as $`\rho`$ increases from
$`0`$ to $`1`$, the final Pass@1 gap typically exhibits a trend of first
increasing and then decreasing, with the largest gain attained at an
interior value of $`\rho`$. These results support
Remark [5.2](#S5.SS2.SSS0.Px2 "Interpreting 𝒩<0. ‣ 5.2 Main Results ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
which predicts that moderate difficulty ratios between adjacent tasks
are the most favorable for easy-to-hard scheduling to realize its
advantage within the iterative self-improvement framework.

## 7 Conclusion

In this work, we developed a task-centric framework for understanding
iterative self-improvement pipelines. For a single task, our
finite-sample analysis characterizes key factors (e.g., task difficulty
and sampling budget) that determine when (multi-step) self-improvement
happens, and it also exposes intrinsic limits on what can be achieved
through self-improvement alone. Beyond single-task training, we provide
theoretical guidance for structuring iteration-wise curricula across
difficulty levels and identify regimes where easy-to-hard scheduling
yields a stronger lower bound guarantee than fixed-mixture training. By
analyzing the feasibility and improvement conditions, we highlight the
role of appropriate adjacent task difficulty ratios and a critical
sample size near which the provably advantageous initialization region
for easy-to-hard undergoes a sharp phase transition. Finally, our
predictions are further supported by experiments on a synthetic shortest
path task and multiple standard mathematical reasoning benchmarks, where
we observe trends consistent with our theory.

## Appendix: Proofs

### A.  Notation and Deferred Definitions

#### A.1  Notation Summary

| Symbol |  Meaning |
|----|----|
| $`q`$ |  question |
| $`a`$ |  answer |
| $`s(q,a)`$ |  reward; $`s(q,a)\in[0,1]`$, where larger values indicate a better answer $`a`$ to the question $`q`$ |
| $`\tau`$ |  acceptance threshold; $`\tau\in(0,1]`$ for filtering $`s(q,a)\geq\tau`$ |
| $`\theta,\hat{\theta}`$ |  model parameters; $`\hat{\theta}`$ denotes the empirical model; includes variants with superscripts or subscripts³³ 3 A subscript $`t`$ ($`0\leq t\leq L`$) denotes the model before iteration $`t`$ (or after iteration $`t-1`$). Superscripts $`\mathrm{B}`$ and $`\mathrm{E2H}`$ refer to the baseline and the easy-to-hard curriculum in Section [5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Easy-to-Hard Curriculum and Baseline ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"), respectively. |
| $`p(\cdot)`$ |  question distribution; includes variants with subscripts |
| $`\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)`$ |  answer distribution; conditional distribution induced by $`\theta`$ given $`q`$ |
| $`D_{p,\theta}(q,a)`$ |  $`D_{p,\theta}(q,a)=p(q)\pi_{\theta}(a\mid q)`$ |
| $`V_{p}(\theta)`$ |  expected reward; $`V_{p}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p(\cdot)}\,\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}\big[\,s(q,a)\,\big]`$ |
| $`\alpha(\theta,q)`$ |  per-question acceptance rate; $`\alpha(\theta,q)=\Pr_{a\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid q)}[s(q,a)\geq\tau]`$ |
| $`Z_{p}(\theta)`$ |  global acceptance rate; $`Z_{p}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p(\cdot)}[\alpha(\theta,q)]`$ |
| $`n`$ |  question sampling budget; total number of sampled questions per iteration |
| $`m`$ |  per-question answer budget; number of sampled answers per question (unless otherwise stated, $`m=1`$) |
| $`\nu`$ |  $`\nu=\sqrt{1/n}`$ |
| $`\Pi`$ |  model class; see Thm. [4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ 4.1 Single-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula") for details |
| $`c,\gamma`$ |  reward-acceptance coupling constants; see Assump. [4.3](#S4.Thmtheorem3 "Assumption 4.3. ‣ 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula") for details |
| $`\delta,\delta^{\prime}`$ |  failure probabilities; see Thm. [4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ 4.1 Single-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula") and Cor. [4.4](#S4.Thmtheorem4 "Corollary 4.4. ‣ 4.2 Multi-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula") for details |
| $`c_{\delta},c_{\delta^{\prime}}`$ |  $`c_{\delta}=\sqrt{2\log(|\Pi|\,\delta^{-1})}`$, $`c_{\delta^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\log(\delta^{\prime-1})/2}`$ |
| $`L`$ |  total number of iterations |
| $`\beta^{\prime},\beta`$ |  difficulty separation exponents; controls the difficulty ratios between adjacent tasks; see Assump. [5.1](#S5.Thmtheorem1 "Assumption 5.1. ‣ Difficulty levels. ‣ 5.1 Easy-to-Hard Curriculum and Baseline ‣ 5 Iterative Easy-to-Hard Curriculum for Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula") for details |
| $`\Delta`$ |  difficulty uncertainty width; $`\Delta=\beta-\beta^{\prime}`$ |

Table 5: Common notation used throughout the paper.

#### A.2  Deferred Definitions and Explicit Expressions

For readability, several auxiliary quantities are only referenced in the
main text. Here we collect their explicit definitions and expressions.

###### Definition A.1 ($`\mathcal{I}(a,\nu)`$).

Suppose $`(a,\nu)`$ are chosen such that

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
1-\gamma-\frac{c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}{a}>0\qquad\text{and}\qquad 0<\frac{a\,c_{\delta}\nu}{c\,(a(1-\gamma)-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu)^{3/2}}<\sqrt{\frac{4}{27}}.
``` |  |

Then the equation

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
y(1-y)^{2}=\left(\frac{a\,c_{\delta}\nu}{c\,(a(1-\gamma)-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu)^{3/2}}\right)^{2}
``` |  |

admits two solutions in $`(0,1)`$, denoted by
$`y_{-}(a,\nu)<y_{+}(a,\nu)`$. Set

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
x_{-}(a,\nu)=\frac{c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}{a}+\left(1-\gamma-\frac{c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}{a}\right)\,y_{-}(a,\nu),\qquad x_{+}(a,\nu)=\frac{c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}{a}+\left(1-\gamma-\frac{c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}{a}\right)\,y_{+}(a,\nu).
``` |  |

We define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{I}(a,\nu)\;:=\;\big(x_{-}(a,\nu),\,x_{+}(a,\nu)\big)\ \subset\ \left(\frac{c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}{a},\,1-\gamma\right).
``` |  |

###### Definition A.2 ($`\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}`$ and $`\mathcal{N}`$).

Let $`x_{-}(2^{-\beta},\nu)`$ and $`x_{+}(2^{-\beta},\nu)`$ be the
endpoints of the interval $`\mathcal{I}(2^{-\beta},\nu)`$ defined in
Definition [A.1](#A1.Thmtheorem1 "Definition A.1 (ℐ(𝑎,𝜈)). ‣ A.2 Deferred Definitions and Explicit Expressions ‣ A. Notation and Deferred Definitions ‣ Appendix: Proofs ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")
with $`a=2^{-\beta}`$. Let,
$`a_{0}=L/\sum_{i=1}^{L}i^{-\beta^{\prime}}`$ and
$`a_{L}=\sum_{i=1}^{L}i^{-\beta^{\prime}}/L^{1-\beta^{\prime}}`$. Then,
we define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{M}_{1}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)\;:=\;x_{-}(2^{-\beta},\nu)-V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0}),
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{M}_{2}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)\;:=\;V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})-x_{+}(2^{-\beta},\nu),
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{M}_{3}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)\;:=\;x_{-}(2^{-\beta},\nu)-\Bigg(1-\gamma-\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{a_{0}V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}}\Bigg),
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{M}_{4}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)\;:=\;\Bigg(1-\gamma-\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{a_{0}V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}}\Bigg)-x_{+}(2^{-\beta},\nu),
``` |  |

Next, define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{N}\big(\beta^{\prime},\beta,\nu,V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})\big)\;:=\;-\frac{1}{2}\,(a_{L}-1)(1-\gamma)-\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{\,1-\gamma-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu\,}}\cdot\frac{1-\left(\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{2c\,(1-\gamma-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu)^{3/2}}\right)^{\!L-1}}{1-\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{2c\,(1-\gamma-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu)^{3/2}}}
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hskip 20.00003pt+a_{L}\Bigg[\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{2^{-\beta}(1-\gamma)-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{2c\Big(2^{-\beta}\Big(1-\gamma-\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{a_{0}V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}}\Big)-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu\Big)^{3/2}}\cdot e^{-\beta/L}}
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hskip 42.00003pt+\left(\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{2c\Big(2^{-\beta}\Big(1-\gamma-\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{a_{0}V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}}\Big)-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu\Big)^{3/2}}\right)^{\!L-1}L^{-\beta}\cdot\frac{c_{\delta}\nu}{c\sqrt{a_{0}V_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{0})-c_{\delta^{\prime}}\nu}}\Bigg].
``` |  |

### B.  Proofs for Section [4](#S4 "4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")

#### B.1  Proof of Theorem [4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ 4.1 Single-Step Self-Improvement ‣ 4 Iterative Self-Improvement ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula")

###### Proof.

At iteration $`t`$ we obtain $`n^{(m)}_{t}`$ accepted samples
$`\{(q_{i},a_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n^{(m)}_{t}}\!\sim\!D^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}`$.
Under this scheme, the population joint distribution of accepted pairs
can be written as

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
D^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}(q,a)=p^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}(q)\;p^{\prime}_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(a\mid q),
``` |  |

where the marginal over questions and the conditional over answers are,
respectively,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}(q)=\frac{p_{0}(q)\,\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)}{Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})},\qquad p^{\prime}_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(a\mid q)=\frac{\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(a\mid q)\,\mathbf{1}_{\{\,s(q,a)\geq\tau\,\}}}{\alpha(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)},
``` |  |

where

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\alpha(\hat{\theta}_{t},q):=\Pr_{a\sim\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(\cdot\mid q)}\!\big[s(q,a)\geq\tau\,\big|\,q\big]=\sum_{a}\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(a\mid q)\,\mathbf{1}_{\{\,s(q,a)\geq\tau\,\}},
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t},q):=1-\big(1-\alpha(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)\big)^{m},\qquad Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t}):=\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p_{0}}\!\big[\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)\big].
``` |  |

For any $`m\geq 1`$, the population MLE objective
$`\mathbb{E}_{(q,a)\sim D^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}}\!\big[\log\pi_{\theta}(a\mid q)\big]`$
at iteration $`t`$ achieves its maximum at
$`\theta=\theta^{\star}_{t+1}`$ where

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)=p^{\prime}_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(\cdot\mid q)
``` |  |

for almost every $`q`$. Note that
$`p^{\prime}_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(\cdot\mid q)`$ is independent of $`m`$
and coincides with the conditional distribution over answers induced by
$`D^{\prime}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}`$; hence, by assumption,
$`p^{\prime}_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}(\cdot\mid q)\in\Pi`$.

Define $`A_{q}\coloneqq\{a:\ s(q,a)\geq\tau\}.`$ By construction,
$`\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(A_{q}\mid q)=1`$ and hence
$`\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(A_{q}^{\mathrm{c}}\mid q)=0`$. Further
define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\delta_{t}(q)\coloneqq\Pr_{a\sim\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)}[s(q,a)<\tau]=\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(A_{q}^{\mathrm{c}}\mid q).
``` |  |

By the definition of total variation distance,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\delta_{t}(q)=\big|\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(A_{q}^{\mathrm{c}}\mid q)-\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(A_{q}^{\mathrm{c}}\mid q)\big|\;\leq\;\TV\!\Big(\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q),\,\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)\Big).
``` |  |

For any dominating measure $`\omega`$, it follows that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\TV\!\Big(\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q),\,\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)\Big)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\Bigg|\frac{\mathrm{d}\,\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)}{\mathrm{d}\omega}-\frac{\mathrm{d}\,\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)}{\mathrm{d}\omega}\Bigg|\,\mathrm{d}\omega`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\Bigg(\int\Bigg(\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\,\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)}{\mathrm{d}\omega}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\,\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)}{\mathrm{d}\omega}}\Bigg)^{\!2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\Bigg)^{\!1/2}`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=:~\sqrt{D_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}\Big(\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q),\,\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)\Big)}.`$ |  |

Here $`D_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(\cdot,\cdot)`$ denotes the Hellinger distance.
Taking expectation over $`q\sim p^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}`$
and using the bound above, we obtain

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}}\big[\delta_{t}(q)\big]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}}\!\Big[D_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}\!\big(\pi_{\theta^{\star}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q),\,\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)\big)\Big]\Bigg)^{\!1/2}.`$ |  |

Based on
Lemma [B.1](#A2.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma B.1 ( [ ] ). ‣ B.1 Proof of Theorem ‣ B. Proofs for Section ‣ Appendix: Proofs ‣ A Task-Centric Theory for Iterative Self-Improvement with Easy-to-Hard Curricula"),
with probability at least $`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}}\big[\delta_{t}(q)\big]\;\leq\;\sqrt{\frac{2\log(|\Pi|\,\delta^{-1})}{n_{t}^{\smash{(m)}}}}.
``` |  |

Therefore, w.h.p.,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\bar{\delta}_{t}^{(p_{0})}\;\coloneqq\;\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p_{0}}\!\big[\delta_{t}(q)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{q\sim p^{\prime(m)}_{p_{0},\hat{\theta}_{t}}}\!\Bigg[\frac{Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})}{\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)}\,\delta_{t}(q)\Bigg]\;\leq\;\frac{Z^{(m)}_{p_{0}}(\hat{\theta}_{t})}{\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t})}\,\sqrt{\frac{2\log\!\big(|\Pi|\,\delta^{-1}\big)}{n_{t}^{\smash{(m)}}}},
``` |  |

where
$`\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t}):=\operatorname{ess\,inf}_{q}\alpha^{(m)}(\hat{\theta}_{t},q)`$.
For any fixed $`q`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(\cdot\mid q)}[s(q,a)]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[s(q,a)\,\mathbf{1}_{A_{q}}\,\big|\,q\big]\;+\;\mathbb{E}\big[s(q,a)\,\mathbf{1}_{A_{q}^{\mathrm{c}}}\,\big|\,q\big]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\tau\,\pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t+1}}(A_{q}\mid q)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\tau\big(1-\delta_{t}(q)\big).`$ |  |

Taking expectation over $`q\sim p_{0}`$, with probability at least
````
